\displaystyle:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t>0:\inf_{\Gamma}(1+{\bar{\eta}_{n}}(t))\leq\alpha\text{ or }\|\bar{\eta}_{n}(t)\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}.

(4)

\partial_{t}\eta=v
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely.

We will construct a complete, right-continuous filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on the new probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
given in Theorem
6.1
, to which the noise processes and the solutions are adapted. However, since the solutions at this stage are not regular enough in time to be considered stochastic processes, we rely on the definition of random distribution and its so-called history as introduced in
[
7
]
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{u}}
be a random distribution then we define its history as

(79)

\displaystyle\sigma_{t}({\boldsymbol{u}}):=\bigcap_{s\geq t}\sigma\left(\bigcup_{{\boldsymbol{q}}\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,s)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\{({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{q}})<1\}\cup\mathcal{N}\right),

where
\mathcal{N}=\{\mathcal{A}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A})=0\}.

Let
\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{\prime}
be the
\sigma-
field generated by the random variables
\eta(s),\bar{W}^{1}(s),\bar{W}^{2}(s)
for all
0\leq s\leq t
.
Then we define

(80)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{t}:=\bigcap_{s\geq t}\sigma(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{s}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{N}),\qquad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\sigma(\sigma_{t}({\boldsymbol{u}},v)\cup\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{t}).

This gives a complete, right-continuous filtration
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, on the probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
, to which the noise processes and the solutions are adapted.
The filtrations
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are defined identically by replacing
{\boldsymbol{u}},v,\eta,\bar{W}^{1},\bar{W}^{2}
with
\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n},\bar{v}_{n},\bar{\eta}_{n},\bar{W}^{1}_{n},\bar{W}^{2}_{n}
in the definition above.

Observe that, due to equivalence of laws, we have

\overline{(\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})_{n}}=\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\quad\text{ and thus, }\overline{(\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}=\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Observe also that, thanks to the first equality in (
57
), the strong almost sure convergence of the approximate densities in
L^{4}((0,T)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
implies that

\nabla\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\nabla\rho\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})).

Moreover, as shown in (
43
), we have that

(81)

\displaystyle{\eta}^{*}(t)={\eta}(t)\quad\text{ for any }t<\tau^{\eta},\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-almost surely,}

where, for any given
\alpha
,
\tau^{\eta}
is an
(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
-stopping time given by:

\tau^{\eta}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t>0:\inf_{\Gamma}(1+\eta(t))\leq\alpha\text{ or }\|{\eta}(t)\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}.

The new random variables
\bar{\mathcal{U}}_{n}
also satisfy the weak formulation (
55
) and the uniform bounds found in Lemma
6.1
.
Using these uniform bounds and the convergence result in Theorem
6.1
we can pass
n\to\infty
in (
55
) and prove that
{\boldsymbol{u}},\rho,v,\eta,\eta^{*}
solve the desired weak formulation (
89
). We will only discuss the passage of
n\to\infty
in the stochastic integral in Proposition
6.4
and in the advection term. For the convergence of the advection term in the weak formulation, we show that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}{P_{n}^{f}}(\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\otimes\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}):\nabla{\boldsymbol{q}}\to\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{u}}:\nabla{\boldsymbol{q}}

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s, for any
t\in[0,T]
for any
{\boldsymbol{q}}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))
.
Recall, due to that Theorem
6.1
, we have
\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\to\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}
in
C_{w}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\beta}{\beta+1}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))
almost surely. Now since the embedding
L^{\frac{2\beta}{\beta+1}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\hookrightarrow H^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
is compact, we can deduce that
\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\to\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}
in
L^{p}(0,T;H^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))
,
1\leq p<\infty
almost surely, for example by Lemma B.2 in
[
39
]
. This strong convergence together with the almost sure convergence of
\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}
weakly in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))
to
{\boldsymbol{u}}
gives us the desired result.

Finally, we use the convergences in Theorem
6.1
to establish
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure convergence of the stochastic integrals in the weak formulation, for the fluid momentum equation. To help with this, we first prove the following lemma, which we will use to show convergence of the stochastic integrals.

Lemma 6.3
.

For every positive integer
k
and for almost every
(\tilde{\omega},t)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]
,

(82)

\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\to 0.

Proof.

This follows by an argument as in
[
7
]
involving Egorov’s theorem and uniform estimates. To apply Egorov’s theorem, the
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure convergences
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
(strongly) in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\rightharpoonup{\boldsymbol{u}}
(weakly) in
L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))
from Theorem
6.1
imply that
\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\to\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}
(strongly)
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))
. So by Egorov’s theorem, there exists for every
\epsilon>0
, a measurable set
A_{\epsilon}\subset\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
such that
|A_{\epsilon}^{c}|\leq\epsilon
, and both
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
and
\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\to\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}
uniformly on
A_{\epsilon}
.

To avoid problems with vacuum, arising from dividing by the square root of the density, we define the set
A_{\epsilon,1}=\{(\omega,t,x)\in A_{\epsilon}:0\leq\rho\leq\epsilon\}
and
A_{\epsilon,2}=A_{\epsilon}\setminus A_{\epsilon,1}
. Then, (
82
) will follow once we observe that:

•

\displaystyle\int_{A_{\epsilon,2}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\to 0\text{ on }A_{\epsilon}^{c}\text{ as $n\to\infty$}
, since we are away from vacuum by the definition of
A_{\epsilon,2}
and by the uniform convergence of
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
and
\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\to\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}
on
A_{\epsilon}
.

•

Next, by (
7
):

\int_{A_{\epsilon,1}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\leq C\int_{A_{\epsilon,1}}(\bar{\rho}_{n}+\bar{\rho}_{n}|\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}|^{2}+\rho+\rho|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2}),

for a constant
C
depending only on
k
, and for sufficiently large
n
, this can be estimated by using the definition of
A_{\epsilon,1}
as:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{A_{\epsilon,1}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\leq 2C\left(\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}(2+|\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}|^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})\right)\epsilon\leq C\epsilon,

for
C
depending only on
k
, where this estimate holds for sufficiently large
n
depending on
(\omega,t,x)
since eventually,
0\leq\rho_{n}\leq 2\epsilon
by the uniform convergence of
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
on
A_{\epsilon}
and the fact that
0\leq\rho\leq\epsilon
on
A_{\epsilon,1}
by definition. We also used the uniform estimate of
\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\in L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
independently of
n
, which follows from Poincaré’s inequality.

•

By uniform bounds of
\bar{\rho}_{n}\in L^{p}(\tilde{\Omega};L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})))
,
\bar{\rho}_{n}|\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}|^{2}\in L^{p}(\tilde{\Omega};L^{2}(0,T;L^{\frac{6\gamma}{4\gamma+3}}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})))
for all
1\leq p<\infty
, the assumption (
7
), and
|A_{\epsilon}^{c}|\leq\epsilon
, for a constant
C
depending only on
k
:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{A_{\epsilon}^{c}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\leq C\limsup_{n\to\infty}\int_{A_{\epsilon}^{c}}(\bar{\rho}_{n}+\bar{\rho}_{n}|\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}|^{2}+\rho+\rho|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{2})\leq C\epsilon^{\frac{2\gamma-3}{6\gamma}}.

So for a constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
and
n
(depending only on
k
) and for arbitrary
\epsilon>0
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\left|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right|^{2}\leq C\epsilon,

which completes the proof.
∎

We now use Lemma
6.3
to show convergence of the stochastic integral as the Galerkin parameter
n\to\infty
. In contrast to the other stochastic integral convergences (see Lemma
5.1
and Lemma
8.7
), we will make use of Egorov’s theorem to establish this convergence, in order to control the term
\rho^{1/2}
in the denominator of the definition (
33
) of
f_{n,k}
, which needs to be appropriately estimated near vacuum. Note that unlike for the convergence argument for the stochastic integral in the time discretization passage
N\to\infty
in Lemma
5.1
, see in particular the estimate (
46
), we do not have bounds of the densities
\rho_{n}
away from vacuum
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely that are independent of the Galerkin parameter
n
. This complicates the argument and requires the use of Egorov’s theorem to rigorously pass to the limit in the stochastic integral term as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma 6.4
.

For
\boldsymbol{q}\in\bigcup_{n}X_{n}
,

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\bar{\eta}_{n}^{*}}}\boldsymbol{F}_{n}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}d\bar{W}_{n}^{1}(t)\to\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}}}\boldsymbol{F}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}d\bar{W}^{1}(t),\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-almost surely.}

Proof.

By using classical ideas
[
1
]
(see Lemma 2.1 of
[
15
]
, Lemma 2.6.6 in
[
7
]
) it suffices to prove

\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta_{n}^{*}}}\boldsymbol{F}_{n}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\to\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}}}\boldsymbol{F}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\text{ in probability in $L^{2}(0,T;L_{2}(\mathcal{U}_{0};{\mathbb{R}}))$},

which follows if we show:

(83)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\Big(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}}}-\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta_{n}^{*}}}\Big)f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{2}\to 0\quad\text{ as $n\to\infty$, for each $k$},

(84)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta_{n}^{*}}}\Big(f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-f_{n,k}(\rho_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{2}\to 0\quad\text{ as $n\to\infty$, for each $k$,}

(85)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\left(\sup_{n}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=m}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}|f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}|+|f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}|\right)^{2}\right)=0.

We observe that (
83
) follows from the
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure strong convergence of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{n}}}\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}}}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))
for any
1\leq p<\infty
. So it suffices to verify (
84
) and (
85
).

Proof of (
84
):
Using Sobolev embedding of
L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})\subset H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
for
l>3/2
:

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\Big(f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\Big)\cdot\boldsymbol{q}\right)^{2}\leq\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\|f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{n})\|_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2},

where we recall from (
33
) that
\displaystyle f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}):=\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]P_{n}^{f}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right)
. This estimate motivates us to show first that

(86)

\|f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})\|_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\to 0,\quad\text{ for {a.e. $(\tilde{\omega},t)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]$}, }

which is established once we obtain these convergences for arbitrary
\psi\in H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
with
l>3/2
:

(1)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]P_{n}^{f}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right)\psi\right|\to 0
for a.e.
(\tilde{\omega},t)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]
.

(2)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}-P^{f}_{n}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right)\right)\psi\right|\to 0
, for a.e.
(\tilde{\omega},t)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]

(3)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\left(f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right)\right)\psi\right|\to 0
for a.e.
(\tilde{\omega},t)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]
.

Proof of statement 1.
By the symmetry of
\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]
with respect to the
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
inner product and by continuity of the projection operator on
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
, for
\psi\in H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
with
l>3/2
:

\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]P_{n}^{f}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right)\psi\right|\leq\left\|\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}-\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\|\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})},

which goes to zero as
n\to\infty
, by estimate (3.15) from
[
8
]
that

(87)

\|\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\rho]\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\leq C\|\psi\|^{1/2}_{H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\|\rho\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{1/2}

and Lemma
6.3
2
2

2

As a technical detail, we emphasize that the constant
C
in estimate (3.14) from
[
8
]
depends only on the Sobolev embedding from
H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
to
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
, and is hence independent of the Galerkin parameter
n
. In particular, we note that the inequality that they use
\|P_{n}\psi\|_{H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\leq\|\psi\|_{H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}
to establish this inequality does not change the constant
C
since the Galerkin basis was also chosen to be orthonormal in
H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
.
. So the result follows from the fact that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\bar{\rho}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}
is bounded for almost every
(t,\bar{\omega})
by the convergence
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely of
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
in
L^{p}(0,T;W^{1,p}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))
for
p>2
.

Proof of statement 2.
This follows analogously to Statement 1, since
\displaystyle P_{n}^{f}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right)\to\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}
in
L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
as
n\to\infty
for almost every
(t,\bar{\omega})
by the fact that
\displaystyle\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
and by properties of the projection operator.

Proof of statement 3.
Finally, for the last convergence, we use symmetry of
\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]
to write

\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\left(f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\left(\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\right)\right)\psi\right|=\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\left(\rho^{1/2}\psi-\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\psi\right)\right|,

which converges to zero as
n\to\infty
for almost every
(t,\tilde{\omega})
for arbitrary
\psi\in H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
(
l>3/2
), by (4.29) from
[
8
]
, which states that given
\bar{\rho}_{n}\to\rho
in
H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
:

\|\mathcal{M}^{1/2}_{n}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\boldsymbol{p}-\sqrt{\rho}\boldsymbol{p}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\to 0\quad\text{ for all $\boldsymbol{p}\in H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})$},

and the fact that
\displaystyle\frac{f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})}{\rho^{1/2}}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
for almost every
(t,\tilde{\omega})
, by (
7
) and uniform estimates.

Conclusion of proof.
Combining the convergences in statements 1-3 implies (
86
) for almost every
(t,\tilde{\omega})
, so the desired convergence (
84
) follows from (
86
) by the Vitali convergence theorem once we show that
\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\|f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})-f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})\|^{2}_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\right)^{r}
is bounded independently of
n
for some uniform constant
C
for some
r>1
. To derive this uniform bound, we use (
7
), (
87
), and the embedding of
H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
into
L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
to estimate:

\displaystyle\|f_{k}(\rho

\displaystyle,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})\|_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}+\|f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})\|_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left[\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}|f_{k}(\rho,\rho{\boldsymbol{u}})|\right)^{2}+\sup_{\|\psi\|_{H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\leq 1}\left|\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}P^{n}_{f}\left(\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right)\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\psi\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{k}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\rho+\rho|{\boldsymbol{u}}|\right)^{2}+\left\|\frac{f_{k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})}{\bar{\rho}_{n}^{1/2}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}\sup_{\|\psi\|_{H^{l}_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\leq 1}\|\mathcal{M}^{1/2}[\bar{\rho}_{n}]\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}

\displaystyle\leq c_{k}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\rho+\rho|{\boldsymbol{u}}|\right)^{2}+c_{k}^{2}\|\bar{\rho}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}\left(\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\bar{\rho}_{n}+\bar{\rho}_{n}|\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}|^{2}\right)

(88)

\displaystyle\leq 4c_{k}^{2}\left(\|\rho\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\rho}{\boldsymbol{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{4}+\|\bar{\rho}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}+\|\bar{\rho}_{n}\|_{L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\bar{\rho}_{n}}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{4}\right).

By using uniform bounds (independent of
n
) on the approximate solutions, this implies that
\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\|f_{k}(\rho,\rho\boldsymbol{u})-f_{n,k}(\bar{\rho}_{n},\bar{\rho}_{n}\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}_{n})\|_{H^{-l}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})}^{2}\Big)^{r}
for
r>1
is bounded independently of
n
for each
k
, which together with (
86
) establishes (
84
). The preceding calculation also establishes (
85
), since
\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}^{2}<\infty
, see (
7
), and since the uniform bounds that we are using to bound the final expression in (
88
) are independent of
n
.

∎

7.
Passing viscosity parameter
\varepsilon\to 0

In this section, to emphasize its dependence on the parameter
\varepsilon
, we denote the solutions constructed on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},(\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
obtained in the previous section by
(\rho_{\varepsilon},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon},\eta_{\varepsilon},\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon},W_{\varepsilon}^{1},W_{\varepsilon}^{2})
respectively. Let us then recall that the weak formulation at this stage reads:

(89)

\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\varepsilon}(t){\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}v_{\varepsilon}(t)\psi=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{p}_{0,\delta,\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}v_{0}\psi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}(\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\otimes{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}):\nabla\boldsymbol{q}\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\Big(a\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma}+\delta\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\beta}\Big)(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{q})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mu_{\delta}^{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\boldsymbol{q}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\Delta{\boldsymbol{q}}\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\lambda_{\delta}^{\eta_{\varepsilon}^{*}}\text{div}({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon})\text{div}(\boldsymbol{q})-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}}}({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}-v_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot(\boldsymbol{q}-\psi\boldsymbol{e}_{z})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\eta_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\eta_{\varepsilon}\Delta\psi-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\psi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}}\boldsymbol{F}(\rho_{\varepsilon},\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}dW^{1}_{\varepsilon}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}G(\eta_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})\psi dW_{\varepsilon}^{2}(t),

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely for any test function
\boldsymbol{q}\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
and for every
t\in[0,T]
.
Moreover, we have that the continuity equation reads
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely as follows,

(90)

\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\text{div}(\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon\Delta\rho_{\varepsilon},\quad\text{ in }\mathcal{O}_{\alpha},\qquad{\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}|_{\partial\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}=0,}\qquad{{\rho_{\varepsilon}(0)=\rho_{0,\delta,\varepsilon}.}}

Thanks to the weak lower semicontinuity of norm, all the uniform bounds obtained Lemma
6.1
, that are also independent of
\varepsilon
, hold at this stage as well for the solution
(\rho_{\varepsilon},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon},\eta_{\varepsilon},\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})
. Hence, most of the calculations in Section
6.1

are valid.
What is different is that we do not have bounds for the spatial derivatives of the density uniform in
\varepsilon
. Hence, the tightness result for the density in
L^{\beta+1}
in space requires a different approach which we explain in the next lemma. This result is required since the uniform energy estimates give us boundedness of
\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\beta}
merely in the non-reflexive space
L^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
which is not sufficient to pass
\varepsilon\to 0
in the pressure term.

Lemma 7.1
.

We have that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma+1}+\delta\rho^{\beta+1}_{\varepsilon})\leq C.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is standard.
Let
\Delta^{-1}\rho
denote the unique solution in
W^{2,\alpha}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\cap W^{1,\gamma^{*}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
, where
\gamma^{*}
is the Holder conjugate of
\gamma
, to the equation

-\Delta w=\rho,\quad w|_{\partial{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}=0.

We set
\psi=0
in (
89
) and apply Ito’s formula to
f_{\chi}(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}})=\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho
in the spirit of Lemma 5.1 in
[
12
]
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}(a\rho^{\gamma+1}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\delta\rho^{\beta+1}_{\varepsilon})=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}_{\delta}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}_{\delta}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}(a\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\gamma}+\delta\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\beta})\nabla\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}(\rho_{\varepsilon}+\nabla\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\otimes{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\otimes{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}:\nabla\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\nabla({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rho_{\varepsilon}):\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\nabla({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\rho_{\varepsilon}):\nabla\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}}f_{k}(\rho_{\varepsilon},\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}dW^{1}_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\varepsilon}}}}({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}-v_{\varepsilon}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot{(\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\nabla\Delta^{-1}\text{div}(\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon})+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\partial_{t}\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho_{\varepsilon}.

The estimates then depend on the fact that, in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, since
\beta>3
, we have the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,\beta}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
which implies that

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{-1}\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\leq\|\rho_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\beta}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}.

Full details of these estimates will be provided for the more difficult proof of obtaining higher integrability of the fluid density uniformly in
\delta
for the
\delta\to 0
limit passage in the next section, see for example the interior pressure estimates of Proposition
8.3
.
∎

7.1.
Tightness of laws

We begin by defining the appropriate spaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}_{\rho}}=C_{w}(0,T;L^{\beta}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))\cap\left(L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))\cap L^{\beta+1}((0,T)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}),w\right)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}_{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}=(L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})),w),\qquad\mathcal{X}_{\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}}=C_{w}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\beta}{\beta+1}}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))\cap C(0,T;H^{-l}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))\quad\text{for }l>\frac{5}{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\eta}=C_{w}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))\cap C(0,T;H^{s}(\Gamma)),\text{ for any }s<2,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{v}=L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))\cap(L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma)),w),\quad\mathcal{X}_{W}=C(0,T;\mathcal{U}_{0})^{2}.

We can then show the following tightness result that follows the proof of Proposition
6.1
and by substituting Lemma
7.1
to obtain tightness of the laws of density.

Proposition 7.1
.

Define the family of random variables

\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}:=(\rho_{\varepsilon},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon},\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon},\eta_{\varepsilon},\eta_{\varepsilon}^{*},v_{\varepsilon},W^{1}_{\varepsilon},W^{2}_{\varepsilon}).

Then the sequence of the laws of
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
i.e. the measures
\{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\circ\mathcal{U}^{-1}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon\geq 0}
is tight in the phase space

{\mathcal{X}}={\mathcal{X}_{\rho}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{\eta}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{\eta}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{v}}\times{\mathcal{X}_{W}}.

7.2.
Skorohod convergence theorem

Hence, by applying the Prohorov theorem and Theorem 1.10.4 in
[
47
]
, which is a variant of the Skorohod representation theorem, we obtain the following convergence result.

Theorem 7.1
.

There exists a filtered probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
and random variables

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}:=(\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\rho_{\varepsilon}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\varepsilon}},\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon}^{*},\tilde{v}_{\varepsilon},\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}^{1},\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}^{2})
, and

{\mathcal{U}}:=(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\tilde{\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}},\eta,\eta^{*},v,\tilde{W}^{1},\tilde{W}^{2})

defined on this new probability space, such that

(1)

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
has the same law in
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}

(2)

\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{U}\text{ in the topology of $\mathcal{X}$, $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely as $\varepsilon\to\infty$},

(3)

\tilde{\eta}^{*}_{\varepsilon}=\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon}
for every
t<\tau^{\eta}_{\varepsilon}
where, for the fixed
s\in(\frac{3}{2},2)
,

\displaystyle\tau^{\eta}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t>0:\inf_{\Gamma}(1+{\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon}}(t))\leq\alpha\text{ or }\|\tilde{\eta}_{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}.

(4)

\partial_{t}\eta=v
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely.

The filtrations
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are constructed as in the previous section ensuring adaptibility of the solutions and that
\tilde{W}^{1},\tilde{W}^{2}
are
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
-Wiener processes and
\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}^{1},\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}^{2}
are
(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
-Wiener processes. The new random variables
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy the continuity equation (
90
) and the structure-fluid momentum equation (
89
) for every
\varepsilon
.

Using the same procedure as in the previous section, we can pass
\varepsilon\to 0
and prove that the random variables
({\boldsymbol{u}},\rho,\eta,\eta^{*})
satisfy the desired weak formulation (
91
) for a fixed
\delta>0
. We refer the reader to the proof of Lemma
8.7
for details regarding passage of
\varepsilon\to 0
in the stochastic integral. We recall here that, due to its construction,
\rho_{0,\delta,\varepsilon}\to\rho_{0,\delta}
in
L^{\gamma}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(see (
21
)) where
\rho_{0,\delta}|_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\setminus{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta_{0}}}=0
.

8.
Passage to the limit in
\delta\to 0

As done previously, to emphasize its dependence on the parameter
\delta
, we denote the probability space and the solutions obtained in the previous section by
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}^{\delta}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})

and
(\rho_{\delta},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\eta_{\delta},\eta^{*}_{\delta},v_{\delta},W^{1}_{\delta},W^{2}_{\delta})
respectively. Let us then recall that the weak formulation that we obtain after the limit in the artificial viscosity parameter
\varepsilon\to 0
states that:

(1)

The following momentum equation,

(91)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\delta}(t){\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}v_{\delta}(t)\psi=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{p}_{0,\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}v_{0}\psi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}(\rho_{\delta}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\otimes{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}):\nabla\boldsymbol{q}\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\Big(a\rho_{\delta}^{\gamma}+\delta\rho_{\delta}^{\beta}\Big)(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{q})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\eta_{\delta}^{*}}_{\delta}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla\boldsymbol{q}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\lambda^{\eta_{\delta}^{*}}_{\delta}\text{div}({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})\text{div}(\boldsymbol{q})\\
&-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}}({\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-v_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot(\boldsymbol{q}-\psi\boldsymbol{e}_{z})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla v_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\psi-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\eta_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\psi\\
&-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\eta_{\delta}\Delta\psi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}\boldsymbol{F}(\rho_{\delta},\rho_{\delta}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}dW^{1}_{\delta}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}G(\eta_{\delta},v_{\delta})\psi dW^{2}_{\delta},\end{split}

holds
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely, for almost every
t\in[0,T]
and for every test function
\boldsymbol{q}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
. We recall that
\boldsymbol{p}_{0,\delta}
, defined in (
20
), approximates the initial data
\boldsymbol{p}_{0}
.

(2)

The continuity equation,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\delta}(t)\phi=\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{0,\delta}\phi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{\delta}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\phi

holds
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely for every
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\bar{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
and
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 8.1

(A discussion about numerology)
.

Many of the chosen constructions in the existence proof, such as extension of viscosity coefficients and the use of the exterior tubular neighborhood
T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}
defined in (
27
) in the penalty term that enforces the kinematic coupling condition in the limit as
\delta\to 0
, rely on
\delta
explicitly in important ways, where the specific numerology is carefully chosen. Before continuing with the existence proof, we remind the reader of the relevant
\delta
-dependent quantities.

First,
\nu_{0}
is a fixed parameter that will later be relevant to the vanishing of density outside the physical domain in the limit as
\delta\to 0
, see Proposition
8.2
. Then, the viscosity coefficients are extended from the physical domain to the maximal domain via the equations (
31
):

\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}=\chi^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta^{\nu_{0}}}\mu,\quad\lambda^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}=\chi^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta^{\nu_{0}}}\lambda,

where
\chi^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta^{\nu_{0}}}
is defined in (
19
), for
\kappa=\delta^{\nu_{0}}
. Note that by the properties of
\chi^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta^{\nu_{0}}}
and of the bounding function
a^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta^{\nu_{0}}}
, see estimate (
16
), we have that

(92)

\displaystyle\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}=\mu,\quad\lambda^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}=\lambda,\quad\text{ for $(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\quad$ such that $z\leq\eta^{*}_{\delta}(x,y)+\left(C_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\delta^{\nu_{0}/2}$},

(93)

\displaystyle\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}=\delta^{\nu_{0}},\quad\text{ for $(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\quad$ such that $z\geq\eta^{*}_{\delta}(x,y)+\left(3C_{\alpha}+\frac{3}{4}\right)\delta^{\nu_{0}/2}$}.

for a constant
C_{\alpha}
that depends only on
\alpha
. More generally, note that

(94)

\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\geq\delta^{\nu_{0}}\text{ and }\lambda^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\geq\delta^{\nu_{0}},\quad\text{ for all }(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}.

Recall also that the penalty term will give a tubular neighborhood estimate
T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}
, see the estimate (
95
), where the tubular neighborhood is defined in (
27
) to be an exterior tubular neighborhood of width
\delta^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}\right)}
containing the points
(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\setminus\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}
such that

0<(z-1-\eta^{*}_{\delta})<\delta^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}\right)}.

We now make an important observation relating the width of the tubular neighborhood to the width of extension for the viscosity coefficients. Since
\nu_{0}
will be chosen to be
\sim\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}\right)^{2}

(cf. (
134
) and Proposition
8.2
)
and since we are considering
\delta
sufficiently small in the limit as
\delta\to 0
, we have that the width of the tubular neighborhood is less than the width of the region outside of the moving domain where the viscosity coefficients are still equal to their values; see (
92
), namely:

\delta^{\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta}\right)}\leq\left(C_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}\right)\delta^{\nu_{0}/2}.

Since we have uniform bounds on the dissipation in terms of the extended viscosity coefficient
\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}
, as in Lemma
8.1
(2), we can use this fact to conclude not just boundedness of
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}
but more generally of
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}
.

Uniform boundedness.
Thanks to the fact that the estimates obtained in Lemma
6.1
are independent of
\delta
, we obtain the following uniform boundedness results.

Lemma 8.1
.

For any
p\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C
that is independent of
\delta
such that:

(1)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\rho_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\leq C
.

(2)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\sqrt{\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\leq C
, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\sqrt{\lambda^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\leq C
.

(3)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\eta_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq C
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\eta^{*}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq C
.

(4)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|v_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq C
.

(5)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\sqrt{\rho_{\delta}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\leq C
.

(6)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\rho_{\delta}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\leq C
.

(7)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup{T}^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}\leq C
.

(8)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}<C(\alpha).\qquad\forall 0\leq q<6

(9)

For some
0<\bar{\kappa}<\frac{1}{2}
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sup_{0<h<T}\frac{1}{h^{\bar{\kappa}}}\|\mathcal{T}_{h}v_{\delta}-v_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(h,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}^{2}\right]\leq C
, where
\mathcal{T}_{h}f(t)=f(t-h)
.

Moreover, we have that

(95)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-v_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}\leq C\delta^{\frac{p}{2}},

We will next discuss how Statements (7) and (8) are derived.
Observe that, since
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}=0
on the bottom boundary of
\mathcal{O}_{\eta_{\delta}^{*}}
, we have

\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)|^{2}dz=\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)}\left(\int_{0}^{z}\partial_{z}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,w)dw\right)^{2}dz\\
\leq(1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y))\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}\int_{0}^{z}|\partial_{z}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,w)|^{2}dwdz\leq(1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y))^{2}\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}|{{\partial_{z}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)}}|^{2}dz.

Hence we obtain the following Poincare inequality by using the fact that
\|\eta^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T)\times\Gamma)}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha}
almost surely:

(96)

\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)|^{2}dz\leq C(\alpha^{-2})\int_{0}^{1+\eta^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}|\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)|^{2}dz.

Similar calculations, obtained by considering the bounding function
a^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\kappa}
defined in (
14
) and by noticing that the extension coefficient
\mu_{\delta}^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}
is equal to
\mu
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup{T}^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}
, give us, for some
C>0
independent of
\delta
, that

(97)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,T:H^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup{T}^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}))}\leq C.

Statement (8) follows from Statement (7) after an application of Corollary 2.9 in
[
30
]
. Finally,
using bounds (
97
) in the proof of Lemma
6.2
gives us Statement (9).

8.1.
Tightness of laws

As done in previous sections, we will now show that the sequence of the laws of the approximate solutions is tight in an appropriate phase space. For that purpose, we define the following spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\rho}=C_{w}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})),\qquad\mathcal{X}_{\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}}={(L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})),w^{*})}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}_{{\boldsymbol{u}}}=(L^{2}(0,T;L^{q}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})),w)},\qquad\mathcal{X}_{\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}}=(L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}),w),\quad\mathcal{X}_{div{\boldsymbol{u}}}=(L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})),w)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\eta}=C_{w}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))\cap C([0,T];H^{s}(\Gamma)),\quad{\mathcal{X}_{v}=L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))\cap(L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma),w)},

(98)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{W}=C(0,T;\mathcal{U}_{0})^{2},\quad\mathcal{X}_{\nu}=(L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha};\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{13})),w^{*}),

for fixed
\frac{3}{2}<s<2
and
q<6
.

A short exposition on Young measures.
In the definition of
\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
in (
98
),
\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{13})
denotes the space of probability measures on the space
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
so that
\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
denotes the space for the
Young measures
for the fluid density, the fluid velocity, and the gradient of the fluid velocity. The intuition for Young measures is as follows. If we have a deterministic fluid velocity and fluid velocity giving rise to the pointwise-defined function
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}):[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
, then for each point in spacetime,
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})
has a single value in
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
. However, a Young measure is a rigorous way of dealing with “multi-valued” functions from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
, where the value at each point
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
is a weighted average of possible values in the range
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
.

To represent this weighted average, a
Young measure
in our current context is a measurable map
\nu:[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{13})
, that is a probability measure on the range
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
for each
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
, where this map from
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
to a probability measure associated to
(t,x)
, denoted by
\nu_{t,x}\in\mathcal{P}({\mathbb{R}}^{13})
, is measurable in an appropriate sense, see Definition 2.8.4 in
[
7
]
. In this sense of Young measures representing weighted averages of potential function values, given any function
(\rho,\boldsymbol{u},\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}):[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
that is real-valued (rather than probability measure-valued as for general Young measures), we can associate a natural Young measure
\nu
defined to have a probability-measured value at
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
of

(99)

\nu_{t,x}=\delta_{\small(\rho(t,x),{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,x),\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,x)\small)},

which is the Dirac delta function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
supported at the specific point
(\rho(t,x),{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,x),\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}(t,x))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
. The Dirac delta measure at each
(t,x)
represents the fact that given a genuinely
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
-valued function
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})
, the value of this function at
(t,x)
is a single determined value in
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
rather than a spread of potential values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
. The space of general Young measures considered as measurable maps from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}
to probability measures on
{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
that we consider for
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}})
is the space
\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
defined in (
98
), which has an appropriate weak-star topology of convergence, which can be precisely defined as in pg. 156 on
[
7
]
. Since the precise definition will not be important to the current exposition, we refer the interested reader to pg. 156 of
[
7
]
for the explicit definition of this space, and more generally to Section 2.8 of
[
7
]
for more information about Young measures.

We can now state the main result of this section is stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 8.1
.

Consider the sequence of random variables,

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}:=\left(\rho_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\right)}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\sqrt{\mu^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\sqrt{\lambda^{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla\cdot{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\rho_{\delta}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\eta_{\delta},\eta_{\delta}^{*},v_{\delta},W^{1},W^{2},\delta_{(\rho,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}}})\right)

where the last term
\delta_{(\rho,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})}
is to be interpreted in the sense of a Dirac delta probability measure-valued function as discussed in (
99
), since the random functions
(\rho,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}{\boldsymbol{u}},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}})
for each
\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}
are (single-valued) real-valued functions from
[0,T]\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{13}
. Then the sequence of measures
\{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{-1}\}_{\delta\geq 0}

is tight in the phase space

\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\rho}\times\mathcal{X}_{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}}\times\mathcal{X}_{div{\boldsymbol{u}}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\rho{\boldsymbol{u}}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\eta}\times\mathcal{X}_{\eta}\times\mathcal{X}_{v}\times\mathcal{X}_{W}\times\mathcal{X}_{\nu}.

The proof of this proposition is identical to that of the corresponding statements in Proposition
6.1
. The only novelty is to show the tightness of the Young measures
\delta_{(\rho,\mathbbm{1}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta_{\delta}^{*}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}})}
, which is shown using the compactness criterion in Corollary 2.8.6 in
[
7
]
, Chebychev’s inequality, and the fact that

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\rho_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}+\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}+\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}\nabla{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\right)<\infty,

as in the proof of Proposition 4.4.7 in
[
7
]
.

8.2.
Skorohod convergence theorem

At this stage we apply the Skorohod representation theorem along with the result of
[
25
]
, to obtain the following convergence result.

Theorem 8.1
.

There exists a probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
and random variables
{\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}:=(\hat{\rho}_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\left({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}\right)}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},\sqrt{\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}^{*},\sqrt{\lambda^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}}\nabla\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta},{\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}},\hat{\eta}_{\delta},\hat{\eta}_{\delta}^{*},\hat{v}_{\delta},\hat{W}^{1}_{\delta},\hat{W}^{2}_{\delta},\hat{\nu}_{\delta})
, and

{\mathcal{U}}:=(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\nabla}^{*},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{div}^{*},\boldsymbol{p},\eta,\eta^{*},v,\hat{W}^{1},\hat{W}^{2},\nu)

defined on this new probability space, such that

(1)

\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}
has the same law in
\mathcal{X}
as
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}
,

(2)

\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}\to\mathcal{U}\text{ in the topology of $\mathcal{X}$, $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely as $\delta\to 0$},

(3)

\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}=\hat{\eta}_{\delta}
for every
t<\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}
where, for the fixed
s\in(\frac{3}{2},2)
,

(100)

\displaystyle\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}

\displaystyle:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t>0:\inf_{\Gamma}(1+{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}(t))\leq\alpha\text{ or }\|\hat{\eta}_{\delta}(t)\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}.

(4)

\partial_{t}\eta=v
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely.

In addition, for any Carathéodory function
H:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{9}\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfying the growth condition:

|H(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{Q})|\leq C(1+\rho^{q_{1}}+|{\boldsymbol{u}}|^{q_{2}}+|\boldsymbol{Q}|^{q_{3}})

for some constant
C
and for some
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}\geq 1
, we have that
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely: for any
1<p\leq\min\left(\frac{\gamma}{q_{1}},\frac{6}{q_{2}},\frac{2}{q_{3}}\right)
,

(101)

H(\rho_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta},\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}\nabla\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta})\rightharpoonup\overline{H(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{Q})},\ \ \text{weakly in $L^{p}((0,T)\times\mathcal{O}_{\alpha})$},

where the weak limit is defined using the limiting Young measure:

\overline{H(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\boldsymbol{Q})}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{13}}H(\rho^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime})d\nu(\rho^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime}).

Proof.

This is an application of the usual Skorohod representation theorem, in addition to a generalization of the Skorohod representation theorem to include the weak convergence of Carathéodory functions of random variables with bounded probability moments (see Theorems 2.8.1 and Corollary 2.8.3 in
[
7
]
). The numerology in (
101
) is due to the result of Theorem 2.8.1 and Corollary 2.8.3 in
[
7
]
and the uniform bounds of
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\right)^{p}
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{q}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\right)^{p}
,
q<6
, and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}\right)^{p}
for all
1\leq p<\infty
, independently of
\delta
.
∎

Furthermore, we have that the following equality holds almost surely,

(102)

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{\delta}=\partial_{t}\hat{\eta}_{\delta}\quad\text{ in the sense of distributions}.

Note in particular that,

(103)

\hat{\eta}_{\delta}\to\eta\text{ and }\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\to\eta^{*}\quad\text{ in }C_{w}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))\cap C(0,T;H^{s}(\Gamma)),\text{ for }s\in(3/2,2),

implies that,

(104)

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}_{\delta}\to\eta\text{ and }\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\to\eta^{*}\quad\text{ in }C(0,T;C(\Gamma)),

which further implies that

(105)

\displaystyle{\eta}^{*}(t)={\eta}(t)\quad\text{ for any }t<\tau^{\eta},\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-almost surely.}

where for a given
\alpha
,

(106)

\displaystyle\tau^{\eta}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t>0:\inf_{\Gamma}(1+\eta(t))\leq\alpha\text{ or }\|{\eta}(t)\|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}.

The proof of (
105
) is identical to that of (
43
).

Thanks to Lemma 5.7 in
[
42
]
we conclude that
\tau^{\eta}
is almost surely strictly positive. In particular, given that the deterministic initial data
\eta_{0}
satisfies (
9
), we have

(107)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}[\tau^{\eta}=0]=0.

This gives us the stopping time which is part of our martingale solution; see Definition
3.1
. We will show that the candidate solution
(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}},\eta)
constructed in Theorem
8.1
satisfies the continuity equation (
10
) in the renormalized sense and the momentum equation (
11
) in the weak sense until this stopping time
\tau^{\eta}
.

Recalling the definition of the history of random variables
\sigma_{t}
(
79
), we will next construct a filtration to which these new random variables are adapted. As done in previous sections,
letting
\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}^{\prime}
be the
\sigma-
field generated by the random variables
\eta(s),\hat{W}^{1}(s),\hat{W}^{2}(s)
for all
0\leq s\leq t
, and
\mathcal{N}=\{\mathcal{A}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}|\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{A})=0\}
, we define

(108)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{t}:=\bigcap_{s\geq t}\sigma(\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{s}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{N}),\qquad\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\sigma(\sigma_{t}({\boldsymbol{u}})\cup\sigma_{t}(v)\cup\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{0}_{t}).

This is a complete, right-continuous filtration
(\hat{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, on the probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
, to which the limiting noise processes and the solutions are adapted. The filtration
(\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\delta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for the approximation weak formulation is constructed similarly.

Next, we know that the weak formulation (
91
) is also satisfied by the new random variables
\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}
, i.e., for any pair of deterministic test functions
({\boldsymbol{q}},\psi)\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\times C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
the following equation,

(109)

\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}\hat{v}_{\delta}(t)\psi=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\boldsymbol{p}_{0,\delta}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}+\int_{\Gamma}v_{0}\psi+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}\otimes\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}):\nabla\boldsymbol{q}\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\Big(a\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta}\Big)(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{q})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}:\nabla\boldsymbol{q}-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\lambda^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\text{div}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta})\text{div}(\boldsymbol{q})\\
-\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot(\boldsymbol{q}-\psi\boldsymbol{e}_{z})-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\hat{v}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\psi-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\hat{\eta}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\psi-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\Delta\hat{\eta}_{\delta}\Delta\psi\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta^{*}_{\delta}}}\boldsymbol{F}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta},\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta})\cdot\boldsymbol{q}d\hat{W}_{\delta}^{1}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}G(\hat{\eta}_{\delta},\hat{v}_{\delta})\psi d\hat{W}_{\delta}^{2}(t),

holds
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely and for almost every
t\in[0,T]
.
Furthermore, the continuity equation reads,

(110)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\phi(t)=\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\rho_{0,\delta}\phi(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(\partial_{t}\phi+\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\phi),

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}((0,T);C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))
and
t\in[0,T]
.

8.3.
Vanishing bounds for density outside of the moving domain

We show an estimate that shows that the integral of the density converges to zero in the exterior region
\left(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\right)^{c}
, with an explicit bound on the rate of convergence of this integral, where we recall the definition of the exterior tubular neighborhood from (
27
). Due to the nature of the proof of this estimate, the moving domain involves
\hat{\eta}_{\delta}
rather than
\hat{\eta}_{\delta}^{*}
, so that the resulting estimate holds only up to the stopping time
\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}
defined in (
100
).
This vanishing of density argument takes advantage of the continuity equation (
110
) and is proved in the spirit of Lemma 3.1 in
[
34
]
and Lemma 4.1 in
[
17
]
.

Proposition 8.2
.

For any
0<\nu_{*}<(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})^{2}
there exists a constant
c
independent of
\delta
, such that

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}^{c}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\cap(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}})^{c}(t)}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\leq c\delta^{\nu_{*}},

where the stopping time
\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}
is defined in (
100
).
We also have the pathwise bound

(111)

\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}^{c}\cap(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}})^{c}(t)}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\leq c(\tilde{\omega})\delta^{\nu_{*}},

for a constant
c(\tilde{\omega})
(independent of
\delta>0
) depending only on the outcome
\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}
.

Proof.

We take
g_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)=z-(1+\hat{\eta}_{\delta}(t,x,y)).
Then
g_{\delta}
satisfies,

\partial_{t}g_{\delta}+(\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot\nabla g_{\delta}=0.

Now let

\varphi_{\delta}=\left[\min\left\{\frac{g_{\delta}}{\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})}},1\right\}\right]^{+},

and we use it as a test function in (
110
). This yields

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\varphi_{\delta}(t)-{\rho_{0,\delta}\varphi_{\delta}(0)}=\frac{1}{\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(\partial_{t}g_{\delta}+\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla g_{\delta}),

for all
t\in[0,T]
, by the definition of the tubular neighborhood
T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}
in (
27
).
Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\varphi_{\delta}(t)=\frac{1}{\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})}}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot\nabla g_{\delta},

since the integral
\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}}\rho_{0,\delta}\varphi_{\delta}(0)
is equal to 0, because
\varphi_{\delta}(0)
is supported outside of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta_{0}}
whereas
\rho_{0,\delta}
, by definition in (
20
), is supported inside of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\eta_{0}}
.
Now recall the definition (
100
) which implies that
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\right\|^{p}_{L^{2}(0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta};L^{2}(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}))}\leq c
. Moreover, we have
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\delta^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\beta}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq c
and
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|\nabla g_{\delta}\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq c
for any
p,q<\infty
. Hence, we obtain for some
1<k<2
that

(112)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\frac{1}{\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}-\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\cdot\nabla g_{\delta}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta};L^{k}(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}))}\leq c.

Since
\varphi_{\delta}(t)=1
on
{\mathcal{O}}^{c}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\cap(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}})^{c}(t)
and 0 on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}
, we use Hölder’s inequality and (
112
) to thus obtain for some
\nu_{*}>0
that

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}^{c}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\cap(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}})^{c}(t)}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\frac{1}{\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})}}\int_{T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}(t)}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)g_{\delta}+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}^{c}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}\cap(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}})^{c}(t)}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\sup_{t\in[0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta}]}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}(t)\varphi_{\delta}(t)\leq c\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}(t)}|^{1-\frac{1}{k}}\leq c\delta^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\beta})(1-\frac{1}{k})}\leq\delta^{\nu_{*}}.

The pathwise form of the estimate follows using exactly the same estimates, where we instead observe that by the weak convergences provided by Theorem
8.1
, we have that the norms
\|\delta^{\frac{1}{\beta}}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\beta}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}
,
\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\delta}}(\hat{{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{\delta}-\hat{v}_{\delta}\boldsymbol{e}_{z})\right\|_{L^{2}(0,\tau^{\eta}_{\delta};L^{2}(T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}}))}
, and
\|\nabla g_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{q}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))}
for any
1\leq q<\infty
are
\tilde{{\mathbb{P}}}
-almost surely bounded by a constant depending on the outcome
\tilde{\omega}\in\tilde{\Omega}
.
∎

8.4.
Weak convergence of the pressure.

Our aim is to now pass
\delta\to 0
in this weak formulation and prove that the new random variable
\mathcal{U}
is a candidate solution. Note that the energy estimates found in Lemma
8.1
hold true for the new random variables as well.
The challenge, as is the case of most compressible flow problems is to pass to the limit in the pressure term
a\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta}
. From the uniform bounds, we only have that this quantity is bounded in
L^{p}(\tilde{\Omega};L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})))
but the function space
L^{1}
is not amenable to weak compactness arguments, from just uniform boundedness. The ultimate goal of this subsection is to show weak convergence of the pressures to some limiting function
\bar{p}
, see Proposition
8.6
:

a\hat{\rho}^{\gamma}_{\delta}+\delta\hat{\rho}^{\beta}_{\delta}\rightharpoonup\bar{p}\text{ weakly in }L^{1}(\tilde{\Omega};L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}))).

This will be established if we can show equi-integrability of the pressure uniformly in the parameter
\delta
, as a result of the following classical weak
L^{1}
convergence criterion.

Lemma 8.2

(Dunford-Pettis Theorem)
.

Consider a bounded family of functions
\mathcal{F}
in
L^{1}({\mathcal{O}})
. Then it contains a subsequence that converges weakly in
L^{1}({\mathcal{O}})
if and only if for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\theta>0
such that for every measurable set
E
, with
\mu(E)<\theta
we have

\int_{E}|f|<\epsilon,\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{F}.

Thus, the goal of this subsection will be to show that the pressure functions
a\hat{\rho}^{\gamma}_{\delta}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta}
satisfies the equi-integrability criterion uniformly in
\delta
. To do this, we will have three estimates on the pressure that together will imply equi-integrability. The first two estimates will be on the time-dependent random moving fluid domain
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}_{\delta}^{*}}
, and the last estimate will be on the exterior tubular neighborhood of the random moving fluid domain. We hence show the following estimates
3
3

3

We remark that the remaining exterior portions of the fluid domain in
\mathcal{O}_{\alpha}\setminus(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}\cup T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}})
can be handled using the same ideas in Estimates 1 and 2.
:

Estimate 1.
Given by Proposition
8.3
, this is a higher integrability estimate on the density that will imply equi-integrability of the pressure in the region within
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
, defined for
l>0
:

(113)

A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:(x,y)\in\Gamma,\ l<z<1+\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)-l\}.

Estimate 2.
Given by Proposition
8.4
, we show that the pressure has arbitrarily small integral within
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
sufficiently near enough to the fluid-structure boundary
\partial\mathcal{O}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
. This will be an estimate on the integral of the pressure on the interior boundary set
{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}\cap(A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}})^{c}
.

Estimate 3.
Finally, in Proposition
8.5
, we obtain an estimate on the integral of the pressure in the exterior tubular neighborhood
T^{\delta}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
, and show an explicit bound on the rate of convergence to zero of the integral of the pressure on this exterior tubular neighborhood.

The crucial estimate and fundamental step is to
improve the integrability of the pressure
in the interior of the moving domain, given in Estimate 1 in Proposition
8.3
. This will allow us to interpret the term
a\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta}
in an
L^{p}
space with
p>1
, which will allow us to attain interior equi-integrability of the pressure. This is traditionally done by using the Bogovski operator to construct an appropriate test function for the momentum equation which requires that the fluid domain is at least Lipschitz continuous. However, since the fluid domain in our case is not regular enough, we will follow the ideas that were first introduced, in the fixed irregular domain case, in
[
29
]
and later extended to the time-varying geometries in
[
10
]
. This procedure of obtaining higher integrability of the fluid density in the interior region
A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
is the content of the following proposition on interior pressure estimates.

Proposition 8.3

(Interior pressure estimates)
.

Recall the definition of
A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}
from (
113
). For a sufficiently small
\Theta>0
depending only on
\gamma
, and every
l>0
, there exists a constant
C_{l}
depending only on
l
and
\alpha
(and independent of
\delta
) such that:

(114)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma+\Theta}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta+\Theta})\leq C_{l}.

Proof.

The idea here is to “test” the momentum equation with

(115)

{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\delta,\Theta}=\chi_{\delta}\boldsymbol{p}_{\delta,\Theta},\quad\text{ such that }\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{\delta,\Theta}=\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta},

and where
\chi_{\delta}
is an appropriate compactly supported function that localizes away from the structure boundary so that the penalized boundary term does not come into the picture. We note here that
\Theta
cannot be arbitrarily large (i.e. we do not obtain arbitrarily high integrability of the density) due to low integrability of the advection term.

To that end, we will take

\boldsymbol{p}_{\delta,\Theta}:=\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta},

where
\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}
is the unique solution in
W^{2,\gamma/\Theta}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\cap W^{1,(\gamma/\Theta)^{*}}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
to

-\Delta w=\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta},

where
(\gamma/\Theta)^{*}
denotes the dual exponent to
\gamma/\Theta
satisfying
\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\gamma/\Theta)^{*}}+\frac{\Theta}{\gamma}=1
.

Next, we will construct a smooth random process
\chi_{\delta}
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}
such that
\chi_{\delta}(t)=0
on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\setminus A^{\frac{l}{4}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)
and such that
\chi_{\delta}(t)=1
in
A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)
for almost every
\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}
and
t\in[0,T]
, where the sets
A_{\eta}^{l}
are defined in (
113
). Due to the random motion of the fluid domain, it will be helpful to explicitly construct this process and obtain appropriate bounds for time and space derivatives of
\chi_{\delta}
in terms of
\hat{\eta}_{\delta}^{*}
.
For that purpose, we will first construct an appropriate smooth
deterministic
function
g
on the fixed maximal domain
{\mathcal{O}}_{\delta}
and then squeeze in such a way that the abovementioned conditions for
\chi_{\delta}
are satisfied. The squeezing operator, however, inherits the regularity of the structure displacement via the ALE map. Hence, to obtain a function
\chi_{\delta}
which is smooth enough, we will also spatially regularize the squeezed function.

Hence, let
g\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
be such that
g(x,y,z)=1
when
\frac{l}{2\alpha}\leq z\leq 1-\frac{l}{2\alpha}
and transitions smoothly from 0 to 1 when
\frac{l}{4\alpha}<z<\frac{l}{2\alpha}
and
1-\frac{l}{2\alpha}<z<1-\frac{l}{4\alpha}
.
Then we define

(116)

\begin{split}\zeta_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)&=g\left(x,y,\frac{z}{(1+\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y))}\right),\quad\text{ in }{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)\\
&=0,\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t).\end{split}

Observe that, by construction, we have
\zeta_{\delta}(t,x,y,z)=1
when
\frac{l}{2}\leq z\leq 1+\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)-\frac{1+\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y)}{\alpha}\frac{l}{2}<(1+\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}(t,x,y))-\frac{l}{2}
i.e. in
A^{\frac{l}{2}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)
which contains the set
A^{{l}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)
.

It is easy to see that
\|\zeta_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{\Omega}\times(0,T)\times{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\leq 1
. Moreover, since
H^{1}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Gamma)
for every
1\leq p<\infty
, we have

(117)

\displaystyle\|\nabla\zeta_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq C(l,\alpha)\|\nabla g\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Gamma))}\leq C\|\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))},

and similarly

(118)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\zeta_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq C(\alpha)\|\partial_{z}g\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\|\partial_{t}\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{p}(\Gamma))}\leq C\|\partial_{t}\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma))},

where the constant
C
depends only on
\Gamma
,
\alpha
and
l
.

Observe, that the regularity (
117
) is not enough. Hence we mollify it with a standard 3D mollifier and denote this space mollification by
\chi_{\delta}
. We choose the radius of mollification
\sigma\ll\frac{l}{8}
, appropriately small (depending only on
\alpha
and
l
and not on
\delta
), so that

\chi_{\delta}(t)=1\text{ in }A^{l}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t)\text{ and }\chi_{\delta}(t)=0\text{ in }{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}\setminus A^{\frac{l}{8}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}(t).

Observe, due to the properties of mollification
\|f^{\lambda}\|_{W^{m+k,p}}\leq C\lambda^{-k}\|f\|_{W^{m,p}}
for
k\geq 0
, and (
117
) that for any
p>3
we have

(119)

\begin{split}\|\chi_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}&\leq C\|\chi_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq\frac{C(\alpha)}{l}\|\zeta_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\\
&\leq C(l,\alpha)\|\nabla g\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}(\Gamma))}\\
&\leq C(l,\alpha)\|\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}(\Gamma))},\end{split}

where the constant
C
depends on (negative powers of)
l
and
\alpha
but is independent of
\delta
. Similarly, thanks to (
118
), we can see that

(120)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\chi_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq C\|\partial_{t}\zeta_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{p}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\leq C\|\partial_{t}\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Gamma))},

where the constant
C
depends only on
\Gamma
and
g
.
Moreover, this explicit construction of the process
\chi_{\delta}
ensures that it is
\{\hat{\mathcal{F}}^{\delta}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
-adapted.

We test (
91
) with
{\boldsymbol{q}}_{\delta,\Theta}
i.e. we apply Itô’s formula to
f_{\chi}(\rho,{\boldsymbol{u}})=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}{\boldsymbol{u}}\cdot\chi_{\delta}\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho^{\Theta}
in the spirit of Lemma 5.1 in
[
12
]
and take the structure test function
\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
, to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}(a\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma+\Theta}

\displaystyle+\delta\hat{\rho}^{\beta+\Theta}_{\delta})=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla\chi_{\delta}\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}(a\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\gamma}+\delta\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\beta})\nabla\chi_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\lambda^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\text{div}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}(\chi_{\delta}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}+\nabla\chi_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla\chi_{\delta}\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}f_{k}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta},\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}d\hat{W}_{\delta}^{1}

\displaystyle+{{\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\nabla\Delta^{-1}[\text{div}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})+(\Theta-1)\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\text{div}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}]}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}{\partial_{t}\chi_{\delta}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\cdot\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}.

Here we additionally used the fact that
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{p}_{\delta,\Theta}=\nabla\Delta^{-1}\partial_{t}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}=\nabla\Delta^{-1}[\text{div}(\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})+(\Theta-1)\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\text{div}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}]
.
Now, due to the positivity of the density, the left-hand side term of the equation above is an upper bound for the desired term that we want to bound i.e. the left-hand side term of (
114
). Hence, we will now find bounds for each term that appears on the right-hand side of the equation above by noting that the energy estimates Lemma
8.1
hold for the new random variables
\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\delta}
found in Theorem
8.1
as well.

• We start with the most critical term. Depending on
\gamma>\frac{3}{2}
, we choose
q<6
and
0<\Theta<\frac{(q-2)\gamma-q}{q}
and utilize the bounds in Lemma
8.1
(8). The definition of
\chi_{\delta}
and Lemma
8.1
give us

\displaystyle\Bigg|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}\Bigg|\leq C\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}}\left|\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\otimes\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left|\Big(\|\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}))}^{2}\|\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\frac{q\gamma}{(q-2)\gamma-q}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\Big)\right|\leq C,

The term
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\nabla\Delta^{-1}\text{div}(\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta})
is treated identically. We remark that the assumption on the adiabatic constant
\gamma>3/2
is essential at this step to ensure that,
\Theta>0
and
q<6
satisfying
\Theta<\frac{(q-2)\gamma-q}{q}
can be appropriately chosen in order to improve the integrability of the fluid density.

• Next, by using (
119
), and taking
\Theta<\frac{\gamma}{3}
, which gives us the embedding
W^{1,\frac{\gamma}{\Theta}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla

\displaystyle\chi_{\delta}\otimes\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}^{\Theta}_{\delta}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\nabla\chi_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\nabla\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\hat{\rho}_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\gamma}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}^{{\Theta}}\right)\leq C.

• Similarly, by choosing
\Theta<\frac{\gamma}{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}}\chi_{\delta}\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}:\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left(\|\mu^{\hat{\eta}^{*}_{\delta}}_{\delta}\nabla\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\|\nabla^{2}\Delta^{-1}\hat{\rho}_{\delta}^{\Theta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{\alpha}))}\right)
